Así, en el último año Cuauhtémoc Cárdenas, Rosario Robles, Alejandro Encinas y, en fechas más recientes, Marcelo Ebrard habían dado portazo al perredismo decepcionados por la aproximación de su cúpula tanto al PRI como al PAN, pero todos ellos, o seguían como independientes, o bien preferían (como el caso de Ebrard, ido al socialdemócrata Movimiento Ciudadano) unirse a otras colectividades.
El enfrentamiento electoral PRD-MORENA adquirió en el feudo del DF, donde el moderado Miguel Ángel Mancera era gobernante, el cariz de una guerra fratricida. En el conjunto del país, los lopezobradoristas recolectaron el 8,8% de los votos y sacaron 35 diputados, quedando a remolque de los perredistas (el 11,4% y 56) y desde luego muy lejos del PAN y el PRI, con 108 y 203 representantes, respectivamente. En la capital, en cambio, MORENA capturó 20 de los 66 diputados de la Asamblea Legislativa contra los 17 del PRD, aupándose como la primera fuerza del hemiciclo, así como cinco de las 16 Jefaturas Delegacionales frente a las seis ganadas por el PRD, cuatro de las cuales lo fueron formando lista común con el PT. En la liza individual de partidos, MORENA superó en votos al PRD. En el DF podía hablarse, por tanto, de victoria para López Obrador. Pero en el resto de estados MORENA cosechó unos resultados entre flojos y paupérrimos. Ni siquiera en Tabasco, el terruño de
AMLO
, pudieron los morenistas hacer un buen papel.
9. Rumbo a la tercera candidatura presidencial con el viento a favor
El 20 de noviembre de 2015, en el II Congreso Nacional de MORENA López Obrador fue elegido por aclamación presidente del CEN del partido en lugar de Martí Batres, quien se hizo cargo de la sección partidaria en la Ciudad de México, mientras que Bertha Luján pasó a encabezar el Consejo Nacional y la ex senadora Yeidckol Polevnsky Gurwitz suplió a la anterior en la Secretaría General.

[SRC-71a459cd1d3200fc UNIT 18 | extracted HTML block 18]
AMLO
ya llevaba meses indicando su deseo de intentar el tercer asalto a la Presidencia de México en 2018. "La tercera es la vencida", comentó con optimismo en febrero de 2015.
Sin embargo, hasta entonces, tocaba superar exámenes como el maratón electoral del 5 de junio de 2016 en 13 estados, con 12 gubernaturas, 547 ayuntamientos y 422 escaños locales en juego, y, de nuevo, en la capital federal, que elegía Asamblea Constituyente para acometer una reforma política del DF; esta, entre otras novedades, traería el cambio de nombre de la entidad federativa, que pasaría a llamarse de manera oficial y exclusivamente Ciudad de México. La consolidación del primer puesto en el DF era importante, pero más lo era avanzar sensiblemente en los estados, pues por el momento MORENA era un partido de escasa implantación nacional, en muchos lugares, como sucedía en el norte, prácticamente nula. López Obrador estuvo en la mente de todos en estos comicios, a pesar de que no concurría a ellos. Desde su salida de la gubernatura del DF, el morenista no había desempeñado ningún mandato de elección popular.
Los resultados de las votaciones de 2016 no fueron precisamente faustos para MORENA, que, por lo que se veía, iba a depender exclusivamente del carisma y el empuje personales de su caudillo si pretendía llegar al Ejecutivo de México. En el DF, sin sorpresas, el partido izquierdista ganó la primera posición de la Constituyente con el 32,9% de los votos y 22 diputados. En cuanto a los estados, sus candidatos a gobernador quedaron sin excepción en posiciones zagueras e incluso testimoniales: fueron terceros en Oaxaca, Puebla, Veracruz y Zacatecas, cuartos en Aguascalientes, Durango, Quintana Roo y Tlaxcala, quintos en Chihuahua, Tamaulipas y Hidalgo, y sexto en Sinaloa. En numerosas circunscripciones el PRD y el PAN concurrieron de la mano, convergencia que preludiaba una candidatura presidencial conjunta.
Un balance provisional de las elecciones estatales de 2016 podía ser que el perredismo, tras la escisión de MORENA y la sangría de dirigentes y cargos electos desafectos, estaba aguantando mejor de lo previsto en las urnas. En el DF el lopezobradorismo le ganaba ciertamente en votos, pero no de manera contundente, quedándose el PRD como la segunda fuerza local.
La relación de fuerzas mejoró sustancialmente para MORENA tras las elecciones del 4 de junio de 2017 en seis estados. Uno de ellos fue el Estado de México, cuyo peso demográfico le convertía en plaza crucial de cara a las presidenciales. En este dominio clásico del priísmo la aspirante a gobernador de MORENA, Delfina Gómez Álvarez, noqueó a su adversario del PRD, Juan Manuel Zepeda Hernández, y le pisó los talones al oficialista Alfredo del Mazo Maza, candidato conjunto del PRI, el PVEM, el PANAL y el Partido Encuentro Social (PES). El 33,6% sacado por del Mazo era el porcentaje más exiguo en los anales del PRI mexiquense y los observadores destacaron que López Obrador había dado un hachazo formidable en Edomex. Con todo, Delfina Gómez no dudó en hablar de fraude en su contra.
El resultado electoral en el Estado de México empujó fuertemente hacia arriba a López Obrador, quien no se veía como un candidato presidencial "eterno": la tentativa de 2018 sería la última, prometió. La sombría situación nacional era propicia para su empresa proselitista. La gestión presidencial de Peña Nieto, constantemente atacado y ridiculizado por el izquierdista ("jefe de la banda de Los Pinos, la que más roba en México", "payaso de las cachetadas al que todos ningunean"), suscitaba un rechazo ciudadano creciente por la aparente incapacidad del Gobierno Federal para ganarle la batalla militar al narco y mejorar el clima, terrible en amplias zonas, de inseguridad.
Desde 2006, 200.000 personas habían sido asesinadas ya y varias miles más se encontraban desaparecidas o desplazadas, escalofriante estadística propia de un país en guerra civil. Peña Nieto era embestido también por la subida de la inflación y el encarecimiento de la canasta básica. La ciudadanía estaba irritada por las alzas de los precios de los combustibles y la electricidad, dos suministros esenciales que la reforma energética de 2013, supuestamente, venía a abaratar. Y sin olvidar los escándalos de corrupción que sacudían al priísmo y minaban su credibilidad.
De cara al exterior, las dificultades para
EPN
se multiplicaron por la llegada a la Presidencia de Estados Unidos de
Donald Trump
, quien colocó a su vecino sureño ante el perturbador escenario de una renegociación integral del TLCAN para proteger a los productores estadounidenses de la atribuida competencia desleal del socio mexicano y la construcción de un muro fronterizo inexpugnable para bloquear la inmigración irregular y la penetración de "hombres malos", muro que México, encima, tendría que costear de su propio bolsillo.
Los constantes menosprecios y exigencias de Trump para con México agudizaban la imagen de debilidad de Peña Nieto y espoleaban el mensaje nacionalista de defensa firme de la "soberanía" y la "dignidad" mexicanas voceado por López Obrador. El presidenciable se declaraba listo para hablarle alto y claro a Washington, para desmontar las "mentiras" y neutralizar el "discurso de odio, persecución y racismo" de Trump. Asimismo, López Obrador se mostraba partidario de que el Gobierno mexicano suspendiera la renegociación del TLCAN hasta después de las elecciones de 2018. Al contrario, el equipo de Peña Nieto quería cerrar las negociaciones en acuerdo antes de las votaciones federales.
Justamente, en junio de 2017 López Obrador anunció el lanzamiento de su último ensayo,
Oye, Trump
, un relato de su reciente periplo por varias ciudades de Estados Unidos "para defender a nuestros paisanos y a los migrantes del mundo ante la actitud autoritaria" del nuevo inquilino de la Casa Blanca. En el decimosexto libro del autor no faltaban las propuestas de gobierno de carácter sectorial, contenido presente también en
2018 La salida: Decadencia y renacimiento de México
, publicado meses antes. Entre 2014 y 2016 el político opositor había sacado a la venta otros tres libros:
Neoporfirismo. Hoy como ayer
(cuya portada contraponía las efigies de Porfirio Díaz y Carlos Salinas),
El poder en el trópico
y
Catarino Erasmo Garza Rodríguez ¿Revolucionario o Bandido?
.

[SRC-71a459cd1d3200fc UNIT 19 | extracted HTML block 19]
A lo largo de 2017 López Obrador, cabeza en todas las encuestas de intención de voto, terminó de perfilar su estrategia de cara a la gran prueba electoral de 2018. En junio, durante el III Congreso Nacional del partido, y luego del reclutamiento para MORENA de los ex presidentes perredistas Leonel Godoy y Leonel Cota (a los que en enero de 2018 iba a seguir Marcelo Ebrard, fichaje estrella de la formación izquierdista), el dirigente desestimó, por una cuestión de "valores y principios", la oferta hecha por la presidenta del PRD, Alejandra Barrales Magdaleno, y Miguel Ángel Mancera de acudir juntos a las elecciones federales en un frente amplio de izquierda. Entonces,
AMLO
descartó explícitamente cualquier alianza preelectoral con el PRD, el PRI, el PAN, el PVEM, el PANAL, el PES y el MC, por tratarse de partidos "al servicio del régimen", y solo aprobó una coalición con el PT.
En el IV Congreso Nacional Extraordinario de MORENA, el 20 de noviembre de 2017, López Obrador hizo presentación del
Proyecto Alternativo de Nación 2018-2024
, documento base para un plan de Gobierno abierto al escrutinio y debate de simpatizantes y ciudadanos, los cuales eran invitados al aporte de diagnósticos, ideas y propuestas. Terminado de ajustar como Proyecto 18 y con Alfonso Romo Garza de coordinador general, el documento se estructuraba en cuatro comisiones temáticas, a saber, Economía y Desarrollo, Desarrollo Social, Política y Gobierno, y Educación, Valores, Cultura y Ciencia, y presentaba siete lineamientos generales, cuales eran: Legalidad y erradicación de la corrupción; Combate a la pobreza; Recuperación de la paz; Viabilidad financiera y austeridad; Equidad de género; Desarrollo sostenible y buen vivir; y Reconstrucción nacional.
A continuación, MORENA, el PT de Alberto Anaya Gutiérrez y el PES de Hugo Éric Flores Cervantes formalizaron la coalición Juntos Haremos Historia, con López Obrador de precandidato presidencial conjunto. A pesar de lo asegurado en junio, el morenista accedió a compartir cancha electoral con un partido, Encuentro Social, habitualmente descrito como de derecha religiosa y, por cierto, la antítesis del socialista y estrictamente laico PT.
Este entendimiento fue interpretado como una muestra del creciente pragmatismo de López Obrador y también como el reconocimiento implícito de, a título personal, una cierta proximidad ideológica al evangélico Flores en temas de fe y moralidad. El líder nacional de MORENA se definía a sí mismo como cristiano "en la extensión amplia de lo que significa el cristianismo" y "seguidor de la vida y la obra de Jesucristo". Su postura más bien conservadora en materia de costumbres sociales era sugerida por su ambigüedad o evasivas cuando se le preguntaba por su opinión sobre cuestiones tales como la despenalización del aborto, el matrimonio igualitario y la adopción homoparental.
El 12 de diciembre de 2017 López Obrador se registró como precandidato presidencial ante la Comisión Nacional de Elecciones de MORENA. Desde este momento, la presidencia del CEN del partido quedaba en manos de la secretaria general, Yeidckol Polevnsky. Ahora mismo, el izquierdista era el primero en las encuestas con un 30% de apoyos. El último paso oficial fue la inscripción ante el INE como candidato por la coalición tripartita Juntos Haremos Historia, el 16 de marzo de 2018. En su discurso de registro,
AMLO
vislumbró el advenimiento de la "Cuarta Transformación en la historia de México", transformación que ellos iban a lograr "sin violencia, de manera pacífica, con información y orientación, con la difusión de principios éticos, liberando a nuestros conciudadanos del miedo y la desinformación, y llamando a todas y todos a buscar la reconciliación".
El candidato se refirió también a la necesidad de un "gobierno honesto que represente a todos", de lograr "el renacimiento económico, social político y, sobre todo, moral de nuestro querido México", y de asentar "un auténtico Estado democrático de derecho". Ellos no compartían la máxima de "ni perdón ni olvido": un Gobierno de MORENA acabaría ciertamente con "la corrupción, la impunidad y los lujos en el Gobierno", pero los "integrantes del poder económico y político" del país debían de saber que "no les guardamos rencor y les aseguramos que tras su posible derrota no habrá represalias, ni persecución, ni destierro para nadie". "Declaramos enfáticamente que lo que se necesita es justicia, no venganza, no odiamos a nadie", recalcó el candidato.
En estos momentos, mediados de marzo de 2018, López Obrador superaba holgadamente a sus principales competidores: por la coalición oficialista Todos por México (PRI, PVEM y PANAL), el capitalino José Antonio Meade Kuribreña, uno de los pesos pesados del equipo de Peña Nieto, del que había sido sucesivamente secretario de Relaciones Exteriores, secretario de Desarrollo Social y Secretario de Hacienda y Crédito Público; y, por la coalición Por México al Frente (PAN, PRD y MC), el mexiquense Ricardo Anaya Cortés, ex diputado federal y dos veces presidente de los panistas entre 2014 y 2017. En los muestreos de intención de voto la delantera del morenista oscilaba entre los 20 y los 30 puntos. Para muchos comentaristas, esta abultada ventaja de López Obrador era irreversible, más al no existir en México el sistema de la doble vuelta.
10. Victoria histórica en las presidenciales de 2008
Hasta las elecciones federales del 1 de julio, López Obrador, Morena y Juntos Haremos Historia fueron desgranando las claves de su programa de Gobierno.
El programa electoral incidía en una serie de temas.
En primer lugar, se abogaba por la "austeridad republicana", un ajuste fiscal ligado al ahorro de gastos corrientes del Estado y la reorientación de los recursos públicos a la inversión, sin subir ningún impuesto (y sí bajando algunos, pero sólo en los estados fronterizos con Estados Unidos) y sin emitir deuda. Habría un aumento gradual del salario mínimo, hasta los 171 pesos al final del sexenio, y lo mismo para las pensiones de adultos mayores, que cobrarían mensualidades de 1.500 pesos. Se incrementaría la oferta de gasolina autóctona para reducir las importaciones y rebajar los precios, lo que requería poner en servicio más refinerías. La reforma energética de Peña Nieto sería sometida a una revisión a fondo, anulando y revirtiendo cuanto fuera necesario. Igualmente, se buscaría las autosuficiencias energética y alimentaria, en el caso del agro ofreciendo a los productores locales precios de garantía.
En el angustioso capítulo de la inseguridad, el Estado prestaría atención a las "causas sociales" de la violencia, lo que pasaba por combatir la pobreza y elevar el desarrollo humano de los colectivos vulnerables. Se asumía el principio de la retirada de las Fuerzas Armadas de las misiones de seguridad interna pero sin precipitaciones, mientras se organizase y desplegase un nuevo cuerpo armado, la Guardia Nacional, con la meta de "pacificar" México en tres años. Por otro lado, MORENA hablaba de "erradicar" la corrupción política "dando ejemplo" institucional desde Los Pinos.
El candidato entonó asimismo la defensa firme de los migrantes mexicanos, más en tiempos de muro fronterizo de Trump, aranceles unilaterales a las exportaciones mexicanas de metales al vecino norteño y renegociación forzada del TLCAN, para lo que buscaría establecer con Estados Unidos unas relaciones de cooperación y buena vecindad basadas "en el respeto mutuo". En marzo de 2018 López Obrador y Polevnsky repitieron que MORENA quería que México dispusiera del mejor equipo de negociadores para conseguir que el tratado que sucediera al TLCAN de 1992 fuera lo más favorable posible a los intereses nacionales. Por otro lado y de nuevo, el candidato salió a desacreditar los intentos de establecer una relación simpática entre su plataforma y el régimen de Caracas: él no tenía "nada que ver con el Gobierno de Venezuela", y su "mexicanismo" equivalía a decir "ni chavismo, ni trumpismo", recalcó.
La plataforma lopezobradorista para el período de gobierno 2018-2024 sugería una socialdemocracia pragmática que ni comulgaba con la teoría de la lucha de clases del socialismo ni aspiraba a volver a los esquemas proteccionistas o estatistas del viejo PRI. Al contrario, asumía los principios ortodoxos del libre mercado, el libre comercio, la estabilidad macroeconómica, la disciplina presupuestaria, la flotación del peso y la autonomía del Banco de México, pero conjugadas, eso sí, con el rechazo al "neoliberalismo", las "tendencias privatizadoras" y la "extranjerización" de sectores estratégicos, pues había que priorizar la "producción nacional", el consumo interno y el "crecimiento horizontal" en todo el país. A los guiños tranquilizadores a empresarios e inversores el tabasqueño añadía la promesa de, llegada la hora de acabar con la corrupción y los privilegios de los ex presidentes, "hacer justicia sin venganza ni odio". "No voy a meter a Peña Nieto en la cárcel", aseguró también durante la campaña.
Otras tomas de postura eran: el rechazo a un Gobierno de gran coalición, es decir, abierto a ministros del PRI o el PAN; la creación de los mecanismos legales de la revocación de los mandatos electos y el desafuero de aquellos cargos públicos procesados por corrupción; la provisión de Internet libre y gratuito en todo el territorio nacional; el cierre de las brechas de desigualdad, sobre todo en el ámbito laboral, entre hombres y mujeres; el programa de becas estudiantiles
Jóvenes Construyendo el Futuro
; o la puesta en servicio de un Sistema Aeroportuario del Valle de México no basado en el proyecto del Nuevo Aeropuerto Internacional de México (NAIM), polémico por sus exorbitantes sobrecostes y su impacto medioambiental, sino en la habilitación del Aeropuerto Internacional de Santa Lucía (AISL) a partir de unas instalaciones militares ya existentes.
El 1 de abril de 2018 López Obrador, rezumando optimismo, abrió en Ciudad Juárez, Chihuahua, su tercera campaña presidencial en 12 años y el 27 de junio la cerró en un Estado Azteca, enorme cancha con capacidad para 87.000 espectadores de la Ciudad de México, lleno hasta la bandera y bañado en euforia. En las filas morenistas había ambiente de celebración por anticipado del triunfo electoral. Y no podía ser menos, a tenor de las encuestas, algunas de las cuales otorgaban a López Obrador más del 50% de los votos.
Tras cerrarse las urnas el domingo 1 de julio, el INE publicó el conteo rápido del PREP, que no dejaba dudas sobre la magnitud de la victoria, aplastante, de López Obrador. Al candidato opositor y virtual presidente electo le bastaron estos resultados preliminares para dirigir un primer mensaje desde el cuartel electoral de MORENA en el Hotel Hilton de la capital. Allí, bajo un gran rótulo que decía "Gracias, México, no les voy a fallar", López Obrador agradeció a sus oponentes el rápido reconocimiento de su triunfo en este "día histórico", llamó "a todos los mexicanos a la reconciliación", prometió "erradicar" la corrupción, "que no es un fenómeno cultural, sino el resultado de un régimen en decadencia", y tendió la mano a Trump, ofreciéndole "una relación de amistad y cooperación para el desarrollo, siempre afincada en el respeto mutuo y en la defensa de nuestros paisanos que viven y trabajan honradamente en Estados Unidos". Precisamente, Trump publicó en Twitter un cordial mensaje de felicitación y buena disposición para su flamante par mexicano.

[SRC-71a459cd1d3200fc UNIT 20 | extracted HTML block 20]
A continuación, López Obrador y su comitiva de colaboradores y familiares se trasladaron al Zócalo, donde les esperaban una muchedumbre enfervorizada, lista para escuchar el discurso de la victoria hasta bien entrada la madrugada del lunes 2 de julio. A sus seguidores,
AMLO
les garantizó la aplicación de "los tres principios básicos: no mentir, no robar y no traicionar al pueblo", anunció una "transición con armonía", desarrollada "de manera ordenada, pacífica", y prometió que los cambios iban a ser "profundos, pero con apego al orden legal establecido". "Escucharemos a todos, atenderemos a todos, respetaremos a todos, pero daremos preferencia a los más humildes y olvidados; en especial, a los pueblos indígenas de México. Por el bien de todos, primero los pobres, dijo el orador, que recalcó también: "Bajo ninguna circunstancia, el próximo presidente permitirá la corrupción ni la impunidad. Sobre aviso no hay engaño: sea quien sea, será castigado. Incluyo a compañeros de lucha, funcionarios, amigos y familiares".
El 8 de julio, una vez completados los cómputos distritales, el INE publicó los resultados finales del escrutinio, que concedían la Presidencia a López Obrador con el 53,19% de los votos, habiendo alcanzado la participación el 64,4% del censo. Anaya había quedado segundo con el 22,27%, Meade tercero con el 16,40% y el independiente Jaime Rodríguez Calderón cuarto con el 5,23%. El morenista había sido el más votado en todos los estados salvo Guanajuato, decantado por Anaya. El 8 de agosto, por ultimo, el TEPJF dictaminó la validez de todo el proceso electoral y entregó la constancia de mayoría al ganador, haciéndose oficial su condición de presidente electo.
Los resultados de las elecciones al Congreso de la Unión y de las celebradas en la mayoría de los estados terminaron de dibujar el alcance de la victoria de MORENA, comparable a un tsunami, y la derrota del PRI, calificable de colapso. El próximo Gobierno de López Obrador iba a gozar de mayoría absoluta en los dos hemiciclos federales: en la Cámara de Diputados, los tres partidos de Juntos Haremos Historia sumaban 308 escaños de 500 y en el Senado 69 escaños de 128. De las nueve gubernaturas sometidas a renovación el 1 de julio, MORENA se llevó las de la Ciudad de México (para Claudia Sheinbaum), Chiapas (Rutilio Escandón), Tabasco (Adán Augusto López) y Veracruz (Cuitláhuac García), mientras que su aliado el PES (Cuauhtémoc Blanco) se hizo con la de Morelos. El PAN, por su parte, retuvo los ejecutivos de Guanajuato y Puebla, y arrebató al PRI el de Yucatán, lo mismo que el Movimiento Ciudadano el de Jalisco. En los comicios a los congresos de 27 estados, MORENA fue la fuerza más votada 18, quedo segunda en siete, tercera en uno y cuarta en otro.
(Cobertura informativa hasta 6/8/2018).

About CSIS
The Center for Strategic and International Studies (CSIS) is a bipartisan, nonprofit policy research organization
dedicated to advancing practical ideas to address the world’s greatest challenges.
Thomas J. Pritzker was named chairman of the CSIS Board of Trustees in 2015, succeeding former U.S. senator
Sam Nunn (D-GA). Founded in 1962, CSIS is led by John J. Hamre, who has served as president and chief
executive officer since 2000.
CSIS’s purpose is to define the future of national security. We are guided by a distinct set of values—nonpartisanship,
independent thought, innovative thinking, cross-disciplinary scholarship, integrity and professionalism, and talent
development. CSIS’s values work in concert toward the goal of making real-world impact.
CSIS scholars bring their policy expertise, judgment, and robust networks to their research, analysis, and
recommendations. We organize conferences, publish, lecture, and make media appearances that aim to increase
the knowledge, awareness, and salience of policy issues with relevant stakeholders and the interested public.
CSIS has impact when our research helps to inform the decisionmaking of key policymakers and the thinking
of key influencers. We work toward a vision of a safer and more prosperous world.
CSIS does not take specific policy positions; accordingly, all views expressed herein should be understood to
be solely those of the author(s).

II | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 4 | page 4]

III | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 5 | page 5]
Introduction
President Andrés Manuel López Obrador, known widely by the initials AMLO, aimed to transform
the politics, economy, and security of Mexico. He calls his wide-ranging series of reforms the
“fourth transformation,” placing it on par with other great moments in Mexican history: Mexico’s
independence, the Mexican Revolution, and the founding of the modern Mexican state. AMLO is nothing if
not ambitious, envisaging himself in the same echelon as Mexico’s national heroes Benito Juárez, Porfirio Díaz,
and—AMLO’s personal favorite—Lazaro Cárdenas. But as his sexenio—Mexico’s six-year presidential term—ends
and AMLO has no possibility for reelection, it is time to take stock of his legacy. A full accounting of what
Mexico has experienced and what kind of Mexico AMLO will leave his successor is in dire order. What will
Mexico look like in 2024?
At times, AMLO appears to be bulletproof, though his policies, even through a favorable lens, have had
mixed results at best. At worst, they have proved counterproductive and even hypocritical. He speaks
of environmental stewardship while pouring money into the oil industry and overseeing an uptick in
deforestation. He proclaims a demilitarized approach to security while expanding the military’s influence over
daily life and wielding a new national guard gendarmerie indiscriminately. He harkens to antiestablishment
and populist credentials while centralizing power and defanging independent institutions meant to check the
executive. Indeed, by a staggering array of metrics, from citizen security to rural development to democratic
accountability, AMLO’s policies actively work against his constituents and self-professed policy objectives.
Nevertheless, AMLO’s personal austerity and a persistent rhetoric of anti-corruption mean his approval ratings
remain among the highest in Latin America. Even after an unprecedented 8 percent drop in approval following
his attack on the popular National Electoral Institute (INE), his approval continued to hover at 60 percent.1
AMLO’s policies have, in turn, influenced Mexico’s economic, security, and political context with uneven and, at
times, paradoxical results. Mexico is inattentive to and seemingly uninterested in nearshoring, lacking a defined
1 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 6 | page 6]
strategy for capitalizing on shifting global supply chains and embroiled in a set of unproductive trade disputes
with its North American neighbors. At the same time, the nation is home to a dynamic and vibrant economy,
which has seen growth in key sectors, from traditional manufacturing to emerging digital technologies and
semiconductors. In terms of security, the forecast is bleak, with efforts to curb organized crime deteriorating
to the point of spurring harmful proposals from the U.S. Congress to authorize the use of military force
against Mexico’s cartels. Nevertheless, a glimmer of potential remains, as long-standing linkages between U.S.
and Mexican security forces continue to encourage cooperation in the form of the U.S.-Mexico Bicentennial
Framework for Security, Public Health and Safe Communities.2 Politically, AMLO’s influence has proved corrosive
to Mexican democracy, presiding over the country’s decline in a few short years from “flawed democracy” to
“hybrid regime,” according to the Economist Intelligence Unit.3 However, the opposition AMLO has encountered
to some of his most deleterious political projects suggests a civil society that remains fiercely dedicated to
protecting its hard-won democracy, along with nontrivial checks from both the legislative and judicial branches.
With fewer than two years remaining in his six-year term, a retrospective is warranted to assess the results
of the AMLO years, what is yet to come, and what trends will persist long after López Obrador has exited the
Palacio Nacional. The objective of such an analysis is not to make predictions about who will succeed AMLO
but rather to adumbrate the contours of a post-AMLO Mexico in three important dimensions: economy,
security, and politics.
In short, Mexico in 2024 looks economically stagnant despite the once-in-a-generation opportunity presented
by nearshoring. The nation is weak and debilitated in its fight against ruthless and expanding cartels and
limping more than sprinting as democratic institutions continue to suffer debilitating attacks. Overall, the
Mexico of 2024 appears far more battered and bruised than the Mexico AMLO took the reins of in 2018.
2 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 7 | page 7]
Economy
President López Obrador’s economic policies have been marked by significant variation. On the
one hand, his domestic policy has revolved around a series of social programs that suggest the
government’s improved commitment to providing a social safety net. On the other, during the
pandemic, Mexico was austere in its spending, devoting a fraction of what other large economies in the region
committed to economic stimulus and judiciously avoiding fiscal crises from increased public debt.4 AMLO
has derided “neoliberalism” in all its forms, using this critique as a capacious tent under which to move to
greater nationalization of industries and revitalization of often sclerotic state-run enterprises. AMLO has also
weaponized austerity to dismantle institutions and agencies he does not like.5
To his credit, he successfully negotiated the U.S.-Mexico-Canada Agreement (USMCA) with the administration
under U.S. president Donald Trump, helping to create one of the world’s most modern trade frameworks.
Further, Mexico remains a powerhouse as the second-largest economy in Latin America, with promise in both
traditional and emerging sectors. However, according to the World Bank’s Global Economic Prospects report,
Mexico’s economy is projected to grow just 0.9 percent in 2023, the lowest rate of growth in all Latin America.6
As Mexico continues on the long path to post-pandemic recovery, it has become increasingly apparent that
much of the country’s economic growth is occurring, as one of the authors notes, “in spite of Mexico’s
government’s policies, rather than because of them.”7
Ultimately, restoration of confidence from investors and the business community will influence Mexico’s
economic position after AMLO according to four main themes: the status of energy reform, Mexico’s ability
to take advantage of the nearshoring moment, the success of AMLO in delivering on promises to develop the
country’s southern and rural areas, and Mexico’s future bureaucratic and regulatory composition.
3 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 8 | page 8]
Energy Reform
President López Obrador undoubtedly has a keen appreciation for the centrality of energy to Mexican politics
and civil society. Indeed, he was carried into power in no small part by the outpouring of popular discontent
against the Peña Nieto administration in 2017 as protests erupted against rising gas prices. The spike in
gasoline prices, known as a gasolinazo, was the sharpest rise in Mexico in two decades.8 Overall, prices in
some areas rose by 48 percent as the government under President Enrique Peña Nieto passed a major energy
reform that ended the legal monopoly of Petróleos Mexicanos (Pemex), Mexico’s state-owned oil company.
Pemex holds the dubious title of the world’s most indebted state-run oil firm and is widely reported to lack
the funds or technology to fully develop Mexico’s oil reserves (the country ranks 21st in the world in terms of
crude oil resources).9 Nevertheless, the dramatic effect of the gasolinazo protests served to reinforce lessons
that López Obrador, a long-standing opponent of privatization, had already taken to heart: the energy reforms
of his predecessor must be rolled back, and gas prices must remain stable.
AMLO quickly set about working to realize these goals, placing a hold on new contracts for foreign oil
companies and auditing over 100 of the awards signed under Peña Nieto for corruption.10 At the same time,
in the early months of his presidency, AMLO declared an $8 billion investment for a new Pemex refinery to be
located in his home state of Tabasco, a commitment later supplemented with a $600 million investment from
Chinese state banks.11 Expected to become operational in 2023, the refinery project has already run some $3
billion over budget.12
View of the Mexican oil company PEMEX’s oil complex in Tula, Hidalgo state, Mexico, on January 11, 2018.
Photo Source: Pedro Pardo/AFP/Getty Images
By far the most ambitious project the president has undertaken is a proposed constitutional reform that would
significantly strengthen the state’s role in the energy sector. Under the proposed law, Mexico’s state-owned
power company, the Comisión Federal de Electricidad (CFE), would have a guaranteed 54 percent market
share while limiting the participation of private electricity suppliers. It would also fold the autonomous Energy
Regulatory Commission (CRE) and the National Hydrocarbons Commission (CNH) into the federal government
by making them subordinate to the Ministry of Energy.13
4 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 9 | page 9]
The amendment proved a rallying cry for domestic opposition, which cited inefficiencies within the CFE that,
by some estimates, would increase operating costs by as much as $3 billion a year while increasing the risk
of power outages by as much as 30 percent.14 The amendment also sparked major concerns internationally,
especially among U.S. energy firms, which voiced fears about the stability of their previous investments.
Ultimately, AMLO was unable to muster the political capital to move the initiative forward, as the lower house
of Congress failed to reach the two-thirds supermajority needed to advance a constitutional amendment.15 The
failure of the reform showcased hard limits on AMLO’s power to drive sweeping legislative change in critical
economic sectors, providing a greater degree of clarity for prospective investors and businesses.
However, the defeat of the energy reform amendment was far from an unmitigated victory for opponents. In
April 2022, Mexico’s Supreme Court issued an ambiguous decision on an earlier energy law that gave the CFE
priority access to send its energy to the grid before foreign energy producers.16 Ultimately, AMLO’s handling of
energy policy impelled the United States and Canada to seek joint consultations under the USMCA in July 2022.
Thus far, the question of energy policy has proved a thorny issue, with the specter of a dispute resolution
panel raising lingering questions for Mexico’s domestic industry and external investors alike. It also threatens
to stall nearshoring momentum.
High energy prices coupled with the CFE’s heavy dependency on oil for power generation mean companies
struggle to obtain cost-effective power and meet their environmental, social, and corporate governance (ESG)
targets while investing in Mexico. Furthermore, in the wake of the amendment’s defeat, AMLO appears to have
shifted his attention toward the nationalization of other industries. On April 17, the same day as the vote in the
lower house, the president introduced a reform to the mining law to nationalize Mexico’s lithium industry. The
law was fast-tracked through Congress and approved just two days later.17
THE ENERGY OUTLOOK FOR 2024
The nationalization of lithium will likely set Mexico back in the transition to lower carbon emissions and the
push for climate-smart energy solutions. When coupled with AMLO’s hostility to foreign energy companies,
many of which seek to invest in renewable power sources, Mexico has locked itself into a traditional carbon-
and-oil energy strategy for the near future. Indeed, Pemex is a mainstay of AMLO’s fourth transformation
domestic project. Mexico’s ability to diversify this strategy in 2024 will depend, in part, on the extent to which
investor confidence can be restored by resolving Mexico’s energy uncertainty through USMCA arbitration and
the willingness of AMLO to strenuously oppose future foreign investments in the energy sector.18 Not only does
this policy impair Mexico’s ability to meet clean energy and environmental goals, it also reverses one of the most
positive trends to come out of the 2013 reform, which prompted a windfall of investment in renewable energy
industries.19 Lithium may buck this trend but only if the national lithium company proves capable of rapidly and
efficiently scaling up production, an unlikely prospect as only one lithium mining project, the Chinese-owned
Bacanora Lithium, appears to stand a realistic chance of beginning production by the end of 2023.20
Another key concern in the near term is gasoline price stability at a time of rising global oil prices. While
AMLO will likely do everything in his power to avoid a gasolinazo under his administration, his energy policies
will become increasingly untenable over the medium and long term as the administration pours more funds
into subsidized fuel. A deterioration of dispute resolution procedures under the USMCA will only exacerbate
such a downward trend, potentially exposing Mexico to punitive tariffs and stifling necessary investment
in sectors needed for the energy transition. Thus, AMLO may successfully stave off an energy crisis under
his administration, but absent a significant course correction when a future crisis hits, it may produce even
greater disruptions than those observed in 2017.
5 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 10 | page 10]

bloomberg.com/news/articles/2022-01-26/investment-in-mexican-green-energy-drying-up-trade-groups-warn#xj4y7vzkg.
Nearshoring Movements
Trade tensions and the global economic disruption wrought by the Covid-19 pandemic have caused the U.S.
economy to reduce its dependence on China. Enthusiasm has renewed in the United States and Mexico
around the prospect of nearshoring industries, in effect relocating production, supply, and value chains
from East Asia to the Western Hemisphere.21 Mexico is a natural choice to receive nearshored industries: it
has the most sophisticated manufacturing base in Latin America, a modern free trade agreement with the
United States in the form of the USMCA, and a 1,900-mile shared border that $677 billion of goods cross
each year.22
However, realizing the potential of nearshoring requires more than favorable conditions. Countries must make
a determined effort to convince companies to relocate to their shores. The Mexican government has yet to
produce policy guidance or strategy documents outlining how it plans to approach nearshoring strategically.
As a result, extrication of supply chains from China to Mexico has been more muted than expected. At the
height of U.S.-China trade tensions under the Trump administration, Mexico’s sales to the United States rose
by about 3.4 percent, not an insignificant amount but far from a windfall of growth.23 Following the Covid-19
pandemic and ongoing supply chain struggles, this growth is expected to increase modestly but fall short of
the promise of nearshoring without more substantial reforms and pro-business policies.
Exacerbating these concerns is Mexico’s precipitous decline as a destination for foreign direct investment (FDI).
According to A. T. Kearney, Mexico has dropped out of the top 25 most attractive countries for FDI for the third
consecutive year.24 Investors cite a restrictive climate driven primarily by nationalization efforts in the oil and
energy sectors. The deteriorating security situation throughout Mexico has also contributed to low investor
confidence. Highway and railway robberies have increased under AMLO, imposing a combined economic toll of
over $10 billion a year.25 Not only do resurgent criminal organizations make it riskier for companies interested
in growing their Mexico-based operations, transport robberies further damage Mexico-U.S. supply chains and
increase the risk that products from Mexico will never make it to their international destination.26
6 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 11 | page 11]
Figure 2: Foreign Direct Investment to Mexico

Source: “Información estadística de la Inversión Extranjera Directa,” [Statistical information on foreign direct investment], Government
of Mexico, data set, October 20, 2017, https://datos.gob.mx/busca/dataset/informacion-estadistica-de-la-inversion-extranjera-directa.
The U.S. decision on March 6, 2023, to press forward with USMCA consultations over AMLO’s plans to bar
imports of certain genetically modified agricultural goods, especially corn, further clouds the nearshoring
outlook. Mexico is now embroiled in not one but two dispute resolution processes covering the critical
sectors of energy and agriculture. Furthermore, AMLO appears obstinate in both areas, limiting room for
maneuvering and increasing the probability of consultations ending in a ruling by a formal dispute panel that
Mexico seems unlikely to win.27 Even an arbitral victory would likely become pyrrhic, as Mexico depends
on the United States for 92 percent of its corn imports. Without a plan for substitution, Mexico could face a
nearly $12 billion drop in gross domestic product (GDP) and a loss of around 57,000 jobs.28 In the meantime,
these trade disagreements inject further volatility and dissuade needed investments. Reports that AMLO has
discussed exiting the USMCA with his advisers when pushed by U.S. trade officials only add uncertainty.
However, Mexico’s digital and technology industry appears to have bucked these downward trends to an extent.
Mexico is home to nine unicorns (technology start-ups valued at over $1 billion), making it the one of the leading
hubs for these types of tech companies in Latin America.29 The USMCA chapter on digital trade may also be a
boon to a technology-forward vision of nearshoring, with streamlined intellectual property and data privacy
rules that can open the doors to greater venture capital access for Mexican companies.30 Growing investments in
the critical semiconductor industry are another bright spot following presidential-level commitments at the 2023
North American Leaders’ Summit (NALS) to develop a North American semiconductor corridor and cross-border
semiconductor manufacturing hubs.31 This includes initiatives to build fabrication hubs, enhance transportation
infrastructure, and cultivate technical exchanges between Mexican and U.S. universities—all promising trends
that, with sustained support, could pay dividends for the overall North American economy.
THE NEARSHORING OUTLOOK FOR 2024
Nearshoring to Mexico will almost certainly continue as supply chains reorient to emphasize the local over the
global. However, such progress will be made likely in spite of AMLO’s policies. While overall levels of FDI may be
lukewarm, recent reports of growing investment in manufacturing space, office rentals, and industrial parks suggest
7 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 12 | page 12]
that businesses recognize how global trends and geographic advantages make Mexico an appealing destination
regardless of hostility at the executive level.32 Further progress in the digital sphere also seems likely. The pandemic
accelerated the switch to virtual platforms for many Mexican citizens, though wireless and internet penetration
remains a serious shortcoming as roughly 35 million Mexicans still lack internet access.33 However, without either
a national nearshoring or credible security plan, Mexico will struggle to become a nearshoring powerhouse in
many of the sectors where it has an established advantage, such as textiles, electronics, and machinery. As a result,
optimistic rhetoric around the potential of Mexico to supplant China will likely remain out of reach by 2024, though
a continued drive toward authoritarian control by President Xi Jinping in China and deterioration of U.S.-China
relations will nevertheless prompt some businesses to consider relocation and still others to invest in Mexico as a
means of building resiliency and redundancy into their supply chains.
Southern Mexico
Historically, southern Mexican states have lagged behind their northern counterparts in terms of economic
development.34 The reasons are varied, including lack of access to trade corridors along the border, low
population density, and underinvestment at the federal level. President López Obrador, who himself hails from
the southern state of Tabasco, has made one of his legacy-defining projects a reversal of this trend, aiming
to unlock the economic potential of Mexico’s south. He has done so through several social and development
initiatives that link the south to neighboring Central American states, as well as infrastructure projects such as
the much-vaunted (and much-maligned) Maya Train.
Turning first to AMLO’s social investments, the López Obrador administration has touted the flagship programs
Sembrando Vida and Jóvenes Construyendo el Futuro not only as solutions to domestic development challenges
but also as Mexico’s blueprint for aid to Central America.35 Thus, while the programs have been implemented
nationwide, they have heavily focused on southern Mexico and the Northern Triangle countries of El Salvador,
Guatemala, and Honduras, where the government has reported 40,000 beneficiaries across both programs.36
Within Mexico, Sembrando Vida, a program that gives rural farmers a stipend of 5,000 pesos a month for planting
and caring for trees on their land, is intended to combat rural poverty and address ecological degradation.
Meanwhile, Jóvenes Construyendo el Futuro aims to address youth unemployment by connecting applicants for the
program with job training and apprenticeship programs while providing a monthly stipend of 3,600 pesos. Both
programs claim over 400,000 beneficiaries throughout the country, with a focus on southeastern states.37
While these initiatives have real potential if implemented successfully and accompanied by rigorous monitoring
and evaluation, their effect in southern Mexico has thus far been ambiguous. According to one report from the
World Resources Institute, Sembrando Vida in its first year of operation may have been indirectly responsible
for the deforestation of more than 73,000 hectares across the states of Campeche, Chiapas, Tabasco, Quintana
Roo, Veracruz, and Yucatán.38 Recipients sought to maximize their monetary gains by cutting down forests to
sell for timber before replanting these with seeds granted through the program. Jóvenes Construyendo el Futuro
has gained a reputation as a tool to distribute political patronage. Indeed, one investigation found that in 17 out
of the top 23 municipalities receiving funding under Jóvenes Construyendo el Futuro, more scholarships were
distributed than there were individuals who met the criteria for enrollment in the program. Findings further
revealed that municipalities with the highest rates of youth unemployment were often underrepresented in the
program, raising complaints that program funds were allocated arbitrarily or even fraudulently.39
Beyond social programs like Sembrando Vida and Jóvenes Construyendo el Futuro, López Obrador has sought
to jump-start southern Mexico’s economy through large-ticket infrastructure projects. These include initiatives
8 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 13 | page 13]
like the aforementioned Dos Bocas refinery in Tabasco, as well as the Maya Train. The train, which would cover
nearly a thousand miles of track along Mexico’s southern gulf coast, aims to link major tourist destinations and
serve as an artery for commerce and cargo shipping. Another initiative, the Corredor Interoceánico del Istmo de
Tehuantepec (CIIT), would update railways running from Salina Cruz, Oaxaca, to Coatzacoalcos, Veracruz, and
link these with the Maya Train to increase rail coverage throughout southern Mexico.40
With a planned start date in 2023, the Maya Train is estimated to cost around $10 billion, a roughly 25 percent
increase from projections when AMLO unveiled it in 2018.41 Vocal participation of Indigenous activists in protests
against the train further muddies AMLO’s framing of the project as a reclamation of Mexico’s precolonial heritage, a
theme he has consistently played up, including through a Mayan ceremony conducted at the train’s staking.
When the train becomes fully operational, as early as December of 2023, it will likely deliver benefits for
the economies of the regions located close to the train stations. Increased tourism, reduced travel times,
and easier transport of goods facilitating nearshoring may all furnish benefits, with government estimates
suggesting the project will generate net benefits for Mexico of $10 billion and a 72 percent decrease in
transport time for products along the route, compared to current means.42 However, these should be weighed
carefully against the potential environmental risks and the possibility of leaving behind the poorest regions of
southern Mexico and those most in need of updated infrastructure. Indeed, Chiapas, which is home to 6 of the
15 poorest municipalities in Mexico, is the location of just one stop on the train’s route, while Oaxaca to the
east, home to 8 of the 15, will not be included at all.43 While the CIIT would theoretically service these states,
the proposed industrial parks along the rail line have already drawn criticism from some local communities
alleging they promote an outmoded and exploitative approach to economic development.44
THE SOUTHERN MEXICO OUTLOOK FOR 2024
Southern Mexico may well grow economically, but the key question is whether this growth will be sufficient to keep
pace with the rest of the country during the post-pandemic recovery. AMLO’s social programs will likely continue to
generate yields along the margins but cannot by themselves meaningfully address rural poverty, especially absent a
more rigorous monitoring and evaluation scheme to address concerns of environmental degradation and political
patronage. By 2024 the Maya Train will be operational in some form, which allows the government to claim it has
achieved the goal within the established timeline. However, the train’s security and efficiency, the broader tourism
industry in Mexico, and the train’s ability to act as a dual-use transit platform for manufacturing and industry
remain major questions. There is greater potential, however, if AMLO’s desire to link southern Mexico and Central
America sprouts a more substantial economic policy than planting trees with Sembrando Vida.
At the moment, there are budding signs of a more intentional approach to development in southern Mexico.
Mexico engaged closely with Guatemala to create a “border of opportunity,” modernizing infrastructure
through a number of transit hubs.45 AMLO has also promised to enroll thousands of Guatemalan seasonal
laborers typically employed in the agricultural sector into the Mexican Social Security Institute (IMSS),
providing them with health benefits, pensions, and access to other social security benefits—a step that signals
commitment to supporting economic development on both sides of Mexico’s southern border.46 Another
effort that could have ramifications for economic opportunity in the south would be a revision of Mexico’s
broadband spectrum pricing scheme, which deters competition and is a major hindrance to internet access,
especially in low-density rural areas.47 Growing access to the internet would be in the interest of AMLO’s
Internet Para Todos strategy and grant new opportunities to thousands in southern Mexico.48 However, the
decision to pursue expanded internet access primarily through the CFE, which has no prior experience with
broadband expansion, bodes ill for prospects of a digital revolution under AMLO’s tenure.
9 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 14 | page 14]
State Capacity and Bureaucracy
The final aspect of AMLO’s economic legacy is somewhat more nebulous to chart and includes a constellation
of reforms, capacity-building efforts, and new special interests from within the federal government. Of all the
economic shifts listed, these changes to the character and operations of Mexico’s federal system are perhaps
most likely to endure long after López Obrador leaves office.
Many of these developments are promising, especially in the realm of tax collection. Mexico’s tax-to-GDP ratio
increased by 1.6 percent between 2019 and 2020, no small feat given the massive disruptions caused by the
Covid-19 outbreak.49 Thus far, growth in tax revenues has been driven by a fairly modest tax policy, characterized
by an increased focus on collection rather than real increases in taxes. These gains have allowed Mexico to
deliver on a balanced budget with manageable levels of public debt in 2022.50 Should Mexico adopt higher taxes
in the coming years, this focus on increased state capacity will prove a boon for government revenues.
AMLO’s spending policy during the height of the pandemic is also worthy of close consideration, as he broke
ranks with many of his peers in the region. While several countries spent heavily on social programs during
health-related lockdowns, Mexico was restrained. Indeed, the dramatic rise in social spending and the
corresponding need to make up for sharp budgetary shortfalls has resulted in severe fiscal crises from Chile to
Colombia, typically accompanied by major popular unrest. By contrast, Mexico’s conservative stance allowed
the government to avoid a significant primary deficit in the short term. However, this policy also came at a
cost, as the country had a net loss of around 400,000 businesses in 2020.51 Coupled with an 8.5 percent drop
in GDP during the pandemic, Mexico’s recovery has proved slower than anticipated.
In 2022 the Mexican economy grew by 3 percent, outperforming pessimistic forecasts but ultimately falling
short of what is needed to recoup losses, while 2023 promises even smaller growth rates. The consequences
of AMLO’s fiscal austerity have also fallen unevenly across the government, as López Obrador has systemically
cut budgets and centralized agencies he views as hostile toward his political project. Perhaps the most notable
instance involves the proposed cuts to the INE, which would have hamstrung the agency’s ability to organize
fair and competitive elections. However, AMLO’s budgetary warfare has ranged broadly, including proposals to
consolidate the functions of key transparency watchdogs like the National Institute for Transparency, Access to
Information and Personal Data Protection (INAI) into the Secretariat of the Civil Service.52
A final noteworthy development in Mexico’s institutional landscape under López Obrador has been his efforts to
systematically position the military in development and economic roles. The most notable instance of this came
before AMLO assumed the presidency, when he helped organize a referendum against the proposed construction
of the Mexico City Texcoco Airport. Subsequent responsibility for the construction and maintenance of a new
international airport was handed over to the Secretariat of National Defense (SEDENA). Since then, the new
airport has become AMLO’s darling, despite repeated setbacks and concerns regarding adherence to international
standards for operation.53 The move not only saw the reshuffling of a major infrastructure contract into military
hands but also will likely give SEDENA veto power over future development and infrastructure spending in and
around the airport and Mexico City more broadly. Meanwhile, a state-owned company controlled by the Secretariat
of the Navy (SEMAR) has been entrusted with rehabilitating hundreds of miles of rail lines, including the CIIT.54
Delegation of infrastructure projects to the armed forces has met with resistance, both within SEDENA and
SEMAR, as well as civil society and the opposition in Congress. Nevertheless, the awarding of these contracts is
likely to create strong factions within the military in support of their expanded role, which may subsequently
create a fearsome roadblock to future efforts at privatization. Lastly, recent legislation passed in a midnight
10 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 15 | page 15]
session of the Senate gives the army a portion of the country’s tourism tax to a company established by the
army to run critical infrastructure such as the new airport and the Maya Train.55
STATE CAPACITY AND BUREAUCRATIC OUTLOOK FOR 2024
Mexico appears headed toward a potential fiscal crunch.56 While pet projects come and go, undone just as easily
as they were conceived, the fiscal challenges they create are much harder to solve. Increased spending, especially
on social (noncontributory) pensions, alongside reduced taxation on state-owned enterprises, especially Pemex,
has resulted in a budgetary mismatch thus far allayed by impressive improvements in tax collection.57 In all
likelihood, López Obrador will seek to delay emergence of a significant primary deficit until after 2024. However,
it seems equally unlikely he will take drastic steps to reverse course on fiscal policy by, for instance, significantly
raising taxes or heavily rolling back social spending. AMLO’s signature projects have proved costly, and most have
involved cost overruns. Thus, whatever successor government arrives in 2024 will have an even shorter timeline
with which to make ends meet. An unexpectedly severe recession in the United States could further accelerate
this timeline by dramatically reducing U.S. demand for Mexico’s manufacturing exports. Policymakers, however,
will be assisted by a more competent tax authority, which may yet soften the blow.

11 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 16 | page 16]
The role of the military in spearheading infrastructure projects will present a similar question to a post-AMLO
government. An opposition candidate would face new bureaucratic interests, especially in the transportation
and logistics sectors, which may be viewed increasingly as the military’s purview. More recently, AMLO’s
decision to order SEMAR to conduct a “temporary occupation” of a privately operated rail line along the
Isthmus of Tehuantepec should be cause for even greater concern, suggesting a role for the military not merely
in operating public infrastructure projects but also in the seizure and administration of heretofore private
facilities.58 If such moves become more commonplace, the militarization of Mexico’s economy will only further
weaken investor confidence and depress economic growth.
Significant questions remain as to how entrenched these interests will be by 2024 and what clashes may occur
within the army and navy on matters of professional ethics and the role of the armed forces. If a successor
from Morena helms the 2024 government and continues along the same course, they may empower an armed
forces constituency for years to come. As such, the legacy of institutional decline and alteration will persist
long after AMLO departs.
12 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 17 | page 17]
Security
Mexico’s economic growth and opportunities are inextricably tied to its security environment.59 Here
the prognosis under AMLO and beyond 2024 appears bleak. Each year, Mexico experiences around
30,000 murders, which have risen to record levels during AMLO’s mandate.60 In 2021 the nation
registered 34,000 homicides, double the number in 2015, while 2022 was the most violent year ever recorded
by the National Public Security System.61 According to Bloomberg, Mexico remains home to eight of the world’s
most dangerous cities.62 Insecurity also characterizes the political scene: in the 2021 midterm elections, 100
political assassinations were reported, 35 of which targeted political candidates.63 With a year and a half
remaining in AMLO’s term, homicides on his watch have already equaled those under his predecessor’s
six-year term and have surpassed by 22 percent the total during the six-year term of Felipe Calderón, the
president whose war on drugs AMLO has derided. Simply put, AMLO’s sexenio will be the bloodiest six-year
term in Mexican history.64
Violence associated with conflict against drug cartels claims most of these victims. López Obrador’s “abrazos,
no balazos” (hugs, not bullets) security policy has seen criminal organizations expand their control to claim up
to 40 percent of Mexican territory.65 With Mexico experiencing some of the highest levels of extortion, human
trafficking, human rights violations, and femicides registered in the country’s history, Mexico’s situation under
AMLO displays an alarming trend.66 These patterns present no signs of slowing, as AMLO’s security reforms
have reduced public spending on security, muddied the citizen security landscape, and reduced cooperation
with the United States on security and counternarcotics issues.67
13 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 18 | page 18]
Members of the Mexican army drive on a road a day after an operation to arrest the son of Joaquín “El Chapo”
Guzmán, Ovidio Guzmán, in Culiacán, Sinaloa state, Mexico, on January 6, 2023.
Photo Source: Juan Carlos Cruz/AFP/Getty Imges
Organized Crime
Among the many challenges Mexico faces, organized crime remains one of the most complex and resilient
threats the country has yet to find a successful strategy for addressing. In 2015 there were around 25 cartels
recognized as major criminal groupings—or what the U.S. Department of Justice calls “well-articulated”
criminal groups. By 2019 this figure had grown to 37, which is likely a significant underestimate.68 In fact,
independent observers identified the presence of up to 463 criminal entities on Mexican territory, explaining
that criminal groupings have grown ever smaller following internal fragmentation and the intensification
of violence as a result of the government’s targeting of kingpins.69 At the same time, an underworld
so fragmented and difficult to trace makes it even harder for political leadership to find adequate and
effective measures to counter its activity, resulting in a vicious cycle. Some of these groups, such as Jalisco
New Generation Cartel (CJNG) or the Sinaloa Cartel, are powerful enough to challenge the state, and the
transformation and fragmentation they provoke either within or between each other often causes deadly
episodes of violence.70 Indeed, U.S. Drug Enforcement Administration (DEA) administrator Anne Milgram has
stated that these two groups pose “the greatest criminal threat” ever faced by the United States.71
During the pandemic, the role of criminal groups became twofold. On the one hand, they exacerbated insecurity
as violent attacks on civilians and battles with other groups continued apace. On the other, criminal groups
complemented, or in some cases took on, the role of the state by distributing food and aid and imposing a curfew
to limit the spread of Covid-19. This was the case for the Sinaloa Cartel, which distributed “Chapo dispensas,”
or boxes of supplies with erstwhile cartel leader Joaquín “El Chapo” Guzmán’s face emblazoned on them.72
Government restrictions on mobility and tighter border controls also affected their activity, and as a result new
channels of criminality arose, such as cybercrimes, which are harder for authorities to detect.73 High profits
and low risks for activities such as fraud, piracy, and cybertheft quickly attracted drug cartels and generated
14 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 19 | page 19]
revenue comparable to drug trafficking. Initially lacking the know-how, criminal groups forced programmers to
collaborate or solicited the help of corrupt Mexican officials and police who sold or passed them spyware for a
share of the profits.74
The inflationary pressures that followed Russia’s invasion of Ukraine also presented new opportunities for the
cartels to diversify and expand their activities.75 Organized criminal groups’ illegal economic activity covers
a range from narcotrafficking of coca, marijuana, opioids, and synthetic drugs such as fentanyl, to oil theft,
human trafficking and smuggling, and the water market. The latter, called aguachicol, consists of stealing great
amounts of water for drug cultivation, leaving poor communities without it, and undermining an already
fragile water environment.76
Escalation of violence and criminal activity by organized groups, together with high levels of impunity
have also driven an astounding number of disappearances, another of Mexico’s major problems. Amnesty
International has identified at least 105,000 disappearances and has found 51,000 unidentified bodies
buried in mass graves.77 Recognizing the difficulty in dealing with such an issue, with more than 36,000
disappearances on the National Search Commission list during his mandate, AMLO authorized a visit from the
UN Committee on Enforced Disappearance in 2020.78
Criminal groups also often penetrate local, state, and national politics to influence campaigns and elections.79
Benefiting from absence of government control and an underresourced INE, cartels have engaged in buying
candidacies and meddling in the political process until the candidate takes office.80 Criminal organizations
were also behind the murder of political candidates in the 2021 midterm elections and in municipal elections
around the country.
López Obrador has been criticized for capitulating to the Sinaloa Cartel in the country’s security operations. In 2019
Ovidio Guzmán López, one of “Los Chapitos,” the sons of El Chapo, was arrested only to be released following a
campaign of violence by the cartel known colloquially as the Culiacanazo.81 The cartel interpreted the release as
a sign of weakness from state authorities and a green light to continue their illegal activities. AMLO also visited El
Chapo’s mother in Sinaloa—a highly unusual act for a president, or any political figure for that matter, to do openly.
In January 2023 El Chapito was arrested again and has been held in a maximum-security facility in Mexico City.
How AMLO handles the U.S. extradition request will have repercussions on the illegal activities of the cartel as well
as a security partnership that has been rocked by a series of previous high-profile criminal cases.
AMLO’s actions during the General Salvador Cienfuegos debacle, in particular, gave rise to major questions
about his commitment to security and anti-corruption efforts. The combination of intense pressure to
repatriate Cienfuegos following his arrest in the United States on charges of colluding with organized crime
and his subsequent acquittal contributed to a serious loss of confidence in U.S.-Mexico security cooperation
efforts.82 Finally, the process against Genaro García Luna, head of the Federal Ministerial Police and Mexico’s
Secretariat of Public Security, who was accused of collaboration with drug traffickers, could have a peculiar
political influence. In 2023 a U.S. court found García Luna guilty of secretly receiving millions in bribes.
Although the conviction is unlikely to implicate other top law enforcement officials indicted alongside him, the
outcome of the trial could have an impact on the 2024 elections by hurting the conservative National Action
Party (PAN), of which García Luna was a member.83 Nothing about the verdict will curb the spread of drug
trafficking or address the cartels’ bloodshed in the country.

THE ORGANIZED CRIME OUTLOOK FOR 2024
Violence resulting from conflicts between organized crime and state authorities have only increased under
AMLO’s mandate, indicating a very pessimistic scenario for the country. Lack of a clear majority in the 2024
national elections could further affect the activities of criminal groups through the emergence of new groups
and battles for control of political forces.
In particular, CJNG, Mexico’s strongest criminal group and one of the most prolific in the world, has potential
to develop even further. Although it has not been powerful enough to challenge all the threats to its rule, it
has shown great adaptability and resilience, as evident in its rapid spread throughout Mexico.84 The Sinaloa
Cartel, at the same time, will probably work to increase its power in an attempt to counterbalance CJNG. This
splintered landscape points to a lack of prospects for anything resembling a Pax Mafiosa enforced by a single
predominant criminal actor.85 Indeed, conflict between various criminal factions appears set to increase and
catch thousands of innocents in the crossfire.
Drug traffickers will adapt to new needs, diversify, and expand their activities to strategic regions, hoping to
get control of supply chains and ports critical for receiving materials from Asia. In addition, climate change and
17 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 22 | page 22]
increasing droughts will continue to make aguachicol a central phenomenon for illegal activities of organized
crime groups. Finally, as long as demand for drugs does not decline and Mexico does not find a more effective
strategy, it is hard to imagine cartels’ activity will substantially diminish after AMLO. On the contrary, the
consequences of AMLO’s policies, which have fueled violence, will be difficult to erase in the short term.
Human Security and Human Rights
Increased activity and territorial control by drug cartels and excessive militarization by the government have
created mounting challenges for Mexican citizens, especially for vulnerable populations, including women,
LGBTQ+, and Indigenous communities. Conflicts between the state and criminal groups or between different
cartels often involve collateral damage and produce effects on public security, spreading fear and a sense of
danger. Corruption that favors the illegal trafficking of drugs and other substances permeates different levels of
society, including the judicial system, resulting in a persistent climate of impunity. Insecurity also affects freedom
of expression, limiting critiques and silencing, often brutally, activists and anti-corruption campaigners.
Mexico is one of the most dangerous countries in the world for human rights defenders and journalists, on
par with Syria, Afghanistan, and war zones.86 In 2022 the United Nations Educational, Scientific, and Cultural
Organization (UNESCO) reported 19 deaths of journalists in Mexico, while the Federal Special Prosecutor’s Office
dealt with 3,362 investigations of crimes against journalists in 2021.87 Of these investigations, only around 25 led
to convictions.88 The Committee to Protect Journalists registered 2020 as the worst year for journalists in Mexico:
they received over 692 threats and attacks, and the trend line since has shown little improvement.89
Despite commitments to gender equality and nondiscrimination after Mexico became the first country in
Latin America to adopt a feminist foreign policy for 2020–24, women in Mexico are facing severe difficulties
in seeing their rights fully recognized.90 The promotion of gender equality abroad in Mexican foreign policy
contrasts with alarming domestic statistics regarding women’s rights and security. On the one hand, a historic
2021 court ruling decriminalized abortion.91 But, on the other, with lockdown policies put in place during
the Covid-19 pandemic, violence against women grew exponentially.92 This led the United Nations to declare
a “femicide pandemic” in Mexico, as murders of women reached a daily average of 11 in 2021. In the same
year, 3,700 women were killed, 887 of which were recognized as femicides. In 2020 the number was even
higher, with 1,000 femicides recorded.93 These astronomical numbers are paired with a lack of investigation
and justice, as well as the stigmatization that victims of gender-based violence and their families often face,
contributing to an environment of insecurity, impunity, and discrimination.

Along the same lines, Amnesty International identified Mexico as the country with the second-highest number
of attacks against members of the LGBTQ+ community.94 In 2021 the National Observatory of Hate Crimes
recorded at least 72 killings and disappearances of LGBTQ+ persons.95
On paper, AMLO embraced feminism as a guiding principle of his mandate, but in practice he has been vocal and
active not only in criticizing women’s and LGBTQ+ protests but also in repressing them with tear gas, arbitrary
detentions, and violence aimed at preventing more people from joining the movements. The government has
cut funding both for public shelters for gender-based violence victims on accusations of corruption, without
evidence, and for national women’s institutes and national initiatives that have tried to protect women, especially
in marginalized communities, such as Indigenous women.96 Furthermore, AMLO has sought to claim that Mexico’s
femicide challenge stems from the country’s past adherence to neoliberal policies. Despite his rhetoric, his actions
suggest not only that the protection of women and LGBTQ+ rights is very low on his agenda but also that, on the
contrary, his actions and policies have even intended to undermine a system that was already suffering.
THE HUMAN RIGHTS OUTLOOK FOR 2024
The weaknesses of Mexico’s justice system make the path forward difficult. Lack of resources, endemic
corruption, unwillingness to tackle impunity, and the complicity of state authorities in human rights abuses
highlight that the situation is complex and difficult to disentangle.
The impact is twofold: On the one hand, perpetrators not duly investigated, processed, or charged for their
crimes continue to pursue their activities with impunity. On the other hand, victims who do not receive justice
continue to feel the psychological, economic, and social impact of what they have experienced.97 This creates
a cycle that only exacerbates human insecurity within the country. Unfortunately, without a change in political
will to focus on addressing and improving the justice and asylum systems and on protecting human rights, and
in light of the alarming data regarding human rights violations in Mexico, the outlook through, and even after,
2024 remains bleak. Human rights and protection of vulnerable populations do not seem to be a priority for
the 2024 candidates. Indeed, AMLO has shown that the proper rhetoric can stand in for genuine policies to
improve human rights protections. It is possible, but unlikely, that pressure from the international community,
especially close economic and interdependent partners, together with continuous agitation from civil society
organizations could play a role in encouraging Mexican leaders to tamp down on abuses.
Reorganization of the Security Sector
A consistent theme in AMLO’s leadership to date has been the mismatch between rhetoric and practice. AMLO’s
electoral campaign proclaimed the end of the Mexican army’s involvement in citizen security, with the aim of
making Mexico a pacifist country. Yet AMLO has dutifully cultivated the armed forces’ loyalty while in office. He
increased the army budget by 22 percent and the budget for the marines by 17 percent, and he let the Mexican
19 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024

[SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1 UNIT 24 | page 24]
army, navy, and the new national guard lead most of the country’s antidrug operations.98 He has also placed
people of confidence in leadership positions and diverted resources from other activities to the armed forces.
AMLO’s goal when he came to office was to transform the security forces and establish a national guard, a
body with civilian command that would merge the military and the federal police and serve to target organized
crime and violence. In practice, the force has remained a de facto auxiliary military unit, with 76 percent of its
115,000 members drawn from either SEDENA or SEMAR.99 In 2021 Mexico’s Senate formally acknowledged the
national guard’s military ties, incorporating the force under the Ministry of Defense in September 2022.
AMLO has faced criticism for the inherently contradictory approach of strengthening militarization in Mexico
without evidence it would result in more efficient management of public security and reduce violence.100 Such
critiques have not stopped him from deploying the national guard on the U.S.-Mexico border to deal with
migration, raising concerns from several human rights bodies over the excessive power concentrated in the
hands of the armed forces.
In 2020 AMLO passed a decree that allowed the national guard to patrol the streets of Mexico until the end of
his mandate. A 2022 vote in the lower house extended the decree to 2028 through a constitutional amendment
revealing AMLO’s intent to authorize the use of the military into his successor’s mandate.101 Once more, the
opposition condemned the move, but to no avail.102
